Sustainable Energy Authority of Ireland
SEAI is the expert authority on decarbonising Ireland’s energy system, using robust, objective data and
an evidence -based approach to help inform policy development and energy user behaviours. SEAI
empowers households, businesses, motorists and the public sector to move away from fossil fuels with
practical advice and financial supports. We encourage positive changes in how Ireland uses energy to
deliver a more prosperous, energy efficient and cleaner environment.

SEAI is funded by the Government of Ireland through the Department of Climate, Energy and the
Environment.

Energy in Ireland

Foreword

Understanding how and where we use energy is essential to supporting
our ambitions for Ireland. It’s at the heart of how we achieve a healthier,
secure and more competitive economy and society – one that’s powered
by clean, affordable energy.

SEAI publishes Energy in Ireland each year to provide the definitive data
on Ireland’s energy supply, demand and related emissions. This
publication provides a look back on 2024, with provisional estimates for
2025. This year’s data are particularly signi ficant as we are now drawing
to the end of Ireland’s first carbon budget, our national commitment to a
pathway of reduced emissions. With over 20 years of data now reported by SEAI, this information helps
us understand if we are on track in meeting those c ommitments and allows us to look at trends in
performance and achievements, as we move through the mid -point of this crucial decade.

Since 2018, the baseline year for the national carbon budgets, total energy related emissions have
fallen by 16%, demonstrating that we are making some progress while also growing our population and
economy. It shows that significant reductions are possibl e when backed by strong policy, investment
and public support and participation working together. But it also tells us that we need to pick up the
pace and do a lot more. Importantly we haven’t broken the link between economic development and
fossil fuels in a structural, meaningful way yet and that is a concern.

Ireland’s energy emissions are falling at a rate that is short of what is needed. But the insights from
this report show us what is possible once we put our shoulder to the wheel. In 2024, we had 41% of our
electricity provided by wind, solar and other re newables. We’re making progress, but we must
accelerate delivery of renewables and major infrastructure such as grid enhancements, public
transport and district heating – piping heat from waste and renewable source into people’s homes and
businesses. We al so need to ensure that we are strategic with energy consumption and growth in our
national decision -making. This is critical if we are to make headway with efforts to reduce our energy
demand overall and increase the share of that demand met through renew able technology.

Insights from the report highlight that we have seen growth across a range of renewable technologies.
Ireland’s heat pumps delivered more renewable energy than all its solar farms and rooftop solar PV
combined, and that’s while also recognising the rapid i ncrease in solar PV generation. This is a
significant step forward for heat pump technology which has such an important role to play in meeting
our heat energy needs.

We saw growth in biofuel blending at garage forecourts meaning less emissions from petrol and diesel
cars, but we still need a significant shift away from private car use to more walking and cycling and
public transport. This shift will be easier for some than others. It is critically important that we drive
ahead with projects such as local bus, train and commuter links and the long -awaited Metrolink.

Our journey requires effort from everyone, but it will be worth it. From Government delivering policy,
supports and investment, to industry, commercial and public sectors being strategic with their energy
demand growth and ensuring it is efficient and met with renewables, to each and every one of us
supporting renewable and infrastructural projects for the greater good and making energy efficient

Energy in Ireland

decisions. We all have a part to play. A just transition and helping those least able to make the
necessary changes, must continue to be central to everything we do.

If we succeed, people will be living in warmer, more comfortable homes. They will commute with ease,
walking and cycling in cleaner air or through electrified transport to more energy efficient workplaces.
Our businesses will be more competitive with all of society holding more control and certainty over
energy costs.

At SEAI, we support householders, businesses and communities who want to reduce their reliance on
fossil fuel every year. We will continue to do all we can to support you to play your part in moving to
clean, more secure energy. Our future together will be bright, with so many benefits for our lives, as
we all work together on this movement. There are so many reasons now to make a change. We haven’t
a moment to lose.

William Walsh, CEO

Energy in Ireland

Technical Highlights

Energy -related Emissions
• Ireland’s national energy -related emissions in 2024 were at their lowest level in over 30 years.
• Energy -related emissions in 2024 were 30.9 MtCO 2eq, down 1.5% on 2023 -levels.
• Electricity accounted for 22.5% of energy emissions, transport for 37.7% , and heat for 39.8%.
• 2024 electricity emissions were 6.9 MtCO 2eq, the lowest on record, down 8.3% on 202 3-levels.
• 2024 transport emissions were 11.7 MtCO 2eq, down 1.2% on 202 3-levels.
• 2024 heat emissions were 12.3 MtCO 2eq, up 2.4% on 202 3-levels.
• Energy -related emissions in 2024 were down 16% on 2018 -levels, the carbon budget baseline.

Renewable Energy
• 14.6% of Ireland’s primary energy was renewable in 2024 – the highest value to date.
• Ireland used 1.31 TWh more renewable energy in 2024 than in 2023.
• Just under three -quarters of renewable energy in 2024 came from wind, biodiesel, and biomass.
• Solar PV generation was up 69% and ambient heat capture from heat -pumps was up 19% in 2024.
• In 2024, Ireland’s RES -Overall result was 16.1%, up from 15.2% in 2023 – the highest value to date.
• Ireland’s RES -Overall result is now above its 2020 16% baseline target, with a 2030 target of 43%.

Transport Energy
• Overall, 93.0% of Ireland’s transport energy came from fossil fuels in 2024.
• Road and air accounted for 75.7% and 21.5% of transport energy demand, respectively.
• Energy demand from private car use increased by 3.2% in 2024.
• Energy demand from HGV road freight increased by 7.9% in 2024 .
• Energy demand from both private cars and HGVs in 2024 was higher than pre -COVID 2019 -levels.
• 9.0% of energy in road diesel, and 6.0% in road petrol , came from renewable biofuels in 2024.

Electricity Supply
• Wind (41.8%), gas (32.1), and net imports (13.9%) led Ireland’s gross electricity supply in 2024.
• Fossil generation accounted for 45.3% of electricity supply in 2024 - the lowest value to date.
• Ireland’s 2024 RES -Electricity result was 41.3%, up from 40.4% in 2023 – the highest value to date.
• Ireland’s RES -Electricity target for 2030 is 80%.

Electricity Demand
• Electricity demand in Ireland was 32.9 TWh in 2024, up 4.1% on 2023 -levels
• Commercial services, which include s the ICT sub-sector , accounted for 41.2% of electricity demand.
• The residential sector accounted for 25.5% of electricity demand in 2024.
• Data centres accounted for 21.2% of all electricity demand in 2024.
• Data centre s account for 88.2% of the increase observed in Ireland’s electricity demand since 2015.

Heat Demand
• Heat demand accounted for 35.1% of Ireland's energy demand in 2024.
• Ireland's heat demand in 2024 was 50.6TWh, up 4.7% on 2023 -levels.
• The residential (44.1%) and industry (36.5%) sectors accounted for over 80% of heat demand.
• In 2024, 89.8% of heat demand was satisfied using fossil fuels.
• Ireland’s residential heat demand in 2024 was 22.3 TWh, up 6.0% on the previous year.


Energy in Ireland

1 Ireland’s Key Energy Trends in 2024 and 202 5

SEAI’s Energy in Ireland 2025 report delivers a comprehensive statistical analysis of Ireland’s energy
system, covering supply, transformation, and end -use demand. It provides clear quantitative
breakdown s of the fuels and economic sectors driving energy -related emissions, the composition of
Ireland’s energy mix, and the supply -streams satisfying our national energy requirements .

Figure 1.1: Key high -level insights on Ireland’s greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, the national
energy mix, and Ireland’s energy import dependency


The publication of this year’s Energy in Ireland report comes at a key moment in Ireland’s energy
transition:

1. National Carbon Budgets - December 2025 marks the end of Ireland’s first national carbon
budget (2021 –2025), with clear sectoral emissions ceilings (SECs) for energy -related emissions in
electricity, transport, built environment , etc.
2. Renewable Energy Directive Targets - the end of 2025 is the precise midpoint of the 2020 –2030
decade in which Ireland has committed to raising its renewable energy share to 43.0%, in line with
the requirements of the third EU Renewable Energy Directive (RED -III).
3. Energy Efficiency Directive Targets – from the end of 2025, Ireland has five years to make sincere
cooperative efforts to limit its total final energy consumption to 10.451 Mtoe by 2030 , under Article
4 of the EU Energy Efficiency Directive (EED) – a reduction of nearly 15% compared to 2024 levels.

SEAI’s Energy in Ireland 2025 report delivers both high -level summaries and detailed data. Section 1
provides a summary of key energy trends and observations from 2 024 and 2025, blending definitive
annual data from the 2024 national energy balance with provisional monthly data from January to
September 2025. Sections 2 to 11 and accompanying Appendices provide more detailed quantitative
descriptions of Ireland’s primary energy, energy transformations, final energy demand, energy -related
emissions, as well as technical details on active energy targets and policy , glossaries, conversion
factors, etc.

As introduced last year, the Energy in Ireland 2025 report now quantifies energy -related emissions data
in terms of mega tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MtCO 2eq). This ensures strong alignment
between SEAI’s reporting of energy -related emissions and the SECs in the national carbon budgets, and
to the EPA’s Greenhouse Gas (GHG) Emissions Inventory, compiled in accordance with IPCC guidelines.
Emissions quantifie d in units of MtCO 2eq provide a fuller assessment of global warming potential,
because they c apture the impact of methane and nitrous oxide, as well as those from carbon dioxide.



Energy in Ireland

1.1 Energy -Related Emissions
Ireland’s national energy -related emissions in 202 4 were 30.9 MtCO 2eq, down 1.5% on the previous
year. Ireland's national energy -related emissions, as reported in the EPA’s Greenhouse Gas (GHG)
Inventory and as used in the carbon budgets, do not include emissions associated with international
aviation or international maritime transport1.

Ireland’s national energy -related emissions in 2024 were at a record low - their lowest level in over 30
years . Energy -related emissions in 2024 were down 16.0% on 2018 levels , the base year for the
calculation of the national carbon budgets and Ireland’s overall 2030 emission reduction target.

Figure 1.2: Time -series of Ireland’s national energy -related emissions since 1990


To gain deeper insights, the t otal energy -related emissions can be broken out into the high -level
climate action plan (CAP) sectors of transport emissions and electricity emissions , as th ose are defined
in the national carbon budgets, and all other remaining ‘heat’ emissions2. Noting that while the
transport and electricity sectors have well defined sectoral emission ceilings (SECs) within the carbon
budgets , there is no explicit target for energy -related emissions or the heat emissions3.

Using this break out, we see that t he largest decrease in annual energy -related emissions between 2018
and 2024 came from electricity . Electricity emissions in 2024 were 6.9 MtCO ₂eq, down 3.3 MtCO ₂eq or
32.1 % on 2018 levels . In contrast, the smallest change in annual energy -related emissions between
2018 and 2024 came from transport. Transport emissions in 2024 were 11.7 MtCO ₂eq, down 0.7
MtCO ₂eq or 5.3% on 2018 levels .

While overall energy -related emissions have fallen steadily in recent years, SEAI’s National Energy
Projections report 2025 indicates that the pace of these reductions is not yet sufficient to deliver on
Ireland’s legally binding carbon budgets, even under the most opt imistic scenarios considered under
SEAI’s modelling.


1 These international emissions are calculated and reported separately in accordance with guidance from United Nations
Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC).
2 All other remaining ‘heat’ emissions are defined as total energy -related emissions, less electricity emissions, less transport
emissions.
3 As per the EPA’s GHG inventory, electricity sector emissions under the carbon budgets are given by the sum of emissions from
(1) public electricity and heat production (IPCC code 1.A.1.a), (2) solid fuel and other energy industries (IPCC code 1.A.1.c ), and (3)
fugitive emissions (IPCC code 1.B.1 & 1.B.2). As a result, the electricity emission in the carbon budgets include no emission s from
auto -producers (including CHP plants), but do include the emissions associated with peat -briquetting, and fugitive emis sions,
such as the emissions associated with the production, processing, transmission, and storage of natural gas, etc.

Energy in Ireland

Figure 1.3: Waterfall plot showing 2018 to 2024 changes in national energy -related emissions


The high -level breakout of Ireland’s national energy -related emissions in 2024 are 6 .9 MtCO 2eq (22.5%)
from electricity, 11.7 MtCO 2eq (37.7 %) from transport , and 12.3 MtCO 2eq (39.8 %) from heat .

Figure 1.4: Breakout of 2024 national energy -related emissions across high -level sectors


In absolute terms, national energy -related emissions in 2024 were down 0.47 MtCO 2eq or 1.5% on the
previous year. However, n ot all energy -related emissions decreased in 2024. While electricity emissions
decreased by 0.63 MtCO 2eq (-8.3%), and transport emissions decreased by 0.14 MtCO 2eq (-1.2%) in
2024, heat emissions increased by 0.29 MtCO 2eq (+2.4 %). For more detail o n changes in heat demand,
see section s 1.5 and 6.2. For more details on energy -related emissions, see section 7.

Figure 1.5: Waterfall plot showing 2023 to 2024 changes in national energy -related emissions




Energy in Ireland

The largest contribut or to decreased energy -related emissions in 2024 was the decrease observed in
electricity emissions. Despite an increas e in electricity demand in 2024 , electricity emissions decreased
to the lowest level on record . Electricity emissions in 2024 reached this record low due to several
factors :

1. An overall decrease in indigenous generation of electricity in Ireland .
2. A decrease in indigenous generation of electricity from fossil fue ls.
3. An increased use of net -imported electricity across international interconnectors with Northern
Ireland and Great Britain , the emissions from which are not co untable towards Ireland’s national
emissions.

Ireland’s gross electricity supply , which is the electricity supply needed to deliver on end -user electricity
demand , increased by 1.46 TWh or 4.2% in 2024. However , Ireland’s indigenous generation of electricity
decre ased by 0.33 TWh or 1.0% in 2024 . Ireland made increased use of net imports across international
interconnectors (IC Electr.) in 2024 to increase supply , with n et imports of electricity up 1.79 TW h or
54.5% compared to the previous year. The generation of electricity from renewables in Ireland
increased by 0.23 TWh in 2024, while the generation of electricity in Ireland from fossil fuel s decreased
by 0.52 TWh. It is this decrease in generation from fossil fuels that resulted in the decrease in electricity
emissions.

Figure 1.6: Waterfall plot showing 2023 to 2024 changes in Ireland’s gross electricity supply


Ireland’s electricity emissions under the national carbon budgets are officially determined by the
Environment Protection Agency (EPA) through the Greenhouse Gas (GHG) Inventory. The EPA have
reported electricity GHG emissions of 9.9 MtCO 2eq, 9.7 MtCO 2eq, 7.6 MtCO 2eq and 6.9 MtCO 2eq for the
first 4-years of the first carbon budget (2021 -2025), respectively. This leaves 5.9 MtCO 2eq of ‘allowable’
emissions for electricity in the first carbon budget.

Based on its monthly surveys of energy suppliers, SEAI can make a provisional extrapolation of
Ireland’s electricity emissions to the end of 202 5. This best estimate indicates 6. 5 MtCO 2eq of electricity
emissions in 202 5, which would be down 6% on 202 4 levels.


Energy in Ireland

Figure 1.7: Reported and estimated electricity sector emissions in the first carbon budget


If SEAI’s estimate for 202 5 proves accurate, then the electricity sector will overshoot its sectoral
emission ceiling ( SEC) of 40 MtCO 2eq by 0.6 MtCO 2eq of emissions . For reference, the electricity sector’s
SEC in the second carbon budget (2026 -2030) is 20 MtCO 2eq – precisely half of that in the first budget –
which is equivalent to a nominal annual emission of just 4.0 MtCO 2eq.

The EPA have reported transport GHG emissions of 11.1 MtCO 2eq, 11.8 MtCO 2eq, 11.8 MtCO 2eq and
11.7 MtCO 2eq for the first 4-years of the first carbon budget (2021 -2025), respectively. This leaves 7.7
MtCO 2eq of ‘allowable’ emissions for transport in the first carbon budget. Based on its monthly surveys
of energy suppliers, SEAI can make a provisional extrapolation of Ireland’s transport emissions to the
end of 202 5. This best estimate indicates 11. 5 MtCO 2eq of transport emissions in 202 5, which would be
down 1% on 2024 levels.

Figure 1.8: Reported and estimated transport sector emissions in the first carbon budget


If SEAI’s estimate for 2025 proves accurate, then the transport sector will overshoot its SEC of 54
MtCO 2eq by 3.8 MtCO 2eq of emissions. For reference, the transport sector’s SEC in the second carbon
budget is 37 MtCO 2eq, which is equivalent to nominal annual emissions of just 7.4 MtCO 2eq.

While SEAI can make extrapolations of electricity and transport emissions to the end of 2024, it cannot
confidently generate an estimate for heat emissions in 202 5. This uncertainty comes from the unknown
impact of the Q4 -2025 heating season on demand, and the relatively poor rate of responses that SEAI
receives from its monthly surveys to solid fuel suppliers. Once its annual survey return s from suppliers
are received, and public administrative data from the EPA, the Central Statistics Office (CSO), and
Revenue Commissioners, becomes available , SEAI will determine Ireland’s 2025 heat emissions and
total energy -related emissions .

For more details on the national carbon budgets and SECs, see section 8.1.2 .

Energy in Ireland

1.2 Supply and Demand of Energy
Ireland’s total primary energy requirement in 2024 was 168.0 TWh, up 2.6% on the previous year. Total
primary energy requirement can be thought of as Ireland’s energy supply . It is the amount of ‘raw’
energy that Ireland needs to satisfy end -user demand across the economy . It includes all the energy
that goes directly to end -users, as well as crude oil that is first processed in Ireland into oil -products,
like diesel and petrol used on our roads, and the gas and coal fed into power plants to generate
electr icity.

Ireland’s total final energy consumption in 2024 was 143.7 TWh, up 2.4% on the previous year. Total
final energy consumption can be thought of as Ireland’s energy demand . It is the amount of energy
directly consumed by end -users in the Irish economy . It excludes all transformation, distribution, and
other losses associated with the processing and delivery of primary energy to final end -user s.

Figure 1.9: Illustration of the relationship between Ireland’s total primary energy requirement
(i.e. energy supply) and Ireland’s total final energy consumption (i.e. energy demand)


Understanding the distinction between energy supply and energy demand, and how they influence
each other, helps interpret the dynamics of Ireland’s energy system and provides a more complete
picture of the national energy landscape .

1.2.1 Energy Supply Trends
Ireland’s 168.0 TWh energy supply in 2024 remains dominated by fossil fuel s. Fossil fuels accounted for
81.3% of the national energy supply in 2024 . However, the contribution of fossil fuel s to the supply mix
is falling slowly - down from 97. 2% in 200 4 and 9 0.5% in 201 4. In 2024, the three largest energy
products contribution to the national energy supply were oil (48.9%) , gas (29.6% ), and renewable
energy (14.6%).




Energy in Ireland

Figure 1.10: Breakout of 2024 energy supply across energy products. The term ‘NRW’ stands for
non-renewable wastes


In absolute terms, Ireland ’s energy supply in 2024 was 4. 19 TWh higher than the previous year. There
were increase s in oil, net imports of electricity across international interconnectors (IC Electr.) , gas, and
renewable energy. These increases were c oupled with decrease s in coal, peat, and non -renewable
wastes (NRW) .

In 2024 , both the use of oil and gas in Ireland’s energy supply increased faster than its renewable
energy supply. The 1.31 TWh increase in renewable energy in 2024 accounted for only 31.2 % of the net
increase in energy supply , with the remaining two-thirds of the net increase satisfied mainly through
increased use of net imports of electricity across international interconnectors and increased use of
fossil fuel s.

Figure 1.11: Waterfall plot showing 2023 to 2024 changes in Ireland’s energy supply. The term
‘NRW’ stands for non -renewable wastes


Ireland’s renewable energy supply in 2024 was 24.5 TWh, up 5.6% on the previous year. Ireland’s
renewable energy supply in 2024 was the highest on record – both in absolute and relative terms. For
more detail ed breakdown of energy supply, see section 3.

Ireland’s renewable energy supply in 2024 came from a broad portfolio of technologies and sustainable
energy products. The three largest sources of renewable energy supply were wind generation of
electricity (47.6%) , the biodiesel blended into the ‘road diesel’ sold in garage forecourts (15.0%), and
biomass (12.0%) . Together, these three sources account for just under three -quarters of all renewable
energy used in Ireland in 2024.

Energy in Ireland

Figure 1.12: Breakout of 2024 renewable energy supply across energy sub -products


The national fleet of heat pumps captur ed renewable ambient heat account ing for 4.9% of Ireland’s
renewable energy supply in 2024 . In 2024, Ireland’s heat pumps deliver ed more renewable energy than
all its solar -farms and rooftop solar PV combined. Heat pumps remain one of the fastest growing
sources of renewable energy in Ireland , with ambient heat capture in 2024 , up 18.7 % on the previous
year.

In absolute terms, Ireland’s renewable energy supply in 2024 was 1.31 TWh higher than the previous
year. There were increases in biodiesel, solar PV, biomass, ambient heat capture from heat -pumps,
bioethanol, renewable waste and biogas. These increases were c oupled with decreases in hydro
generation, wind generation, landfill (LF) gas and solar -thermal heating.

Figure 1.13: Waterfall plot showing 2023 to 2024 changes in Ireland’s renewable energy supply


The largest sub -product increase in renewable energy in 2024 came from biodiesel . Ireland’s biodiesel
supply increased by 0.51 TWh or 16.0 % on the previous year, driven by increased biofuel blending into
road diesel , which increased from 7.9% in 2023 to 9.0% in 2024 . The largest sub -product decrease in
renewable energy in 2024 came from wind generation. Ireland’s electricity generation from wind
decreased by 0.21 TWh or 1 .8% on the previous year , despite installed wind capacity increasing. For
more detailed breakdowns on wind, solar, and other renewable energy suppl y streams , see section s
1.3 and 3.



Energy in Ireland

1.2.2 Energy Demand Trends

Ireland’s total end-user energy demand of 143.7 TWh in 2024 can be broke n out in to sectoral demand.
In 2024, the three economic sectors with greatest energy demand were transport (42.3% ), residential
(21.3%), and industry (17.7%). Combined, the transport and residential sectors account for almost two -
thirds of national energy demand.

Figure 1.14: Breakout of 2024 energy demand across economic sectors


In absolute terms, Ireland’s energy demand in 2024 was 3.32 TWh higher than the previous year. There
were increases in energy demand from all economic sectors, except transport , which was down 0.13
TWh or 0.2% on the previous year. In 2024, t he largest sectoral increase in energy demand came from
the residential sector , which increased by 1.54 TWh or 5.3% on the previous year . The second largest
came from the commercial services sector, which increased by 1.00 TWh or 6.2% on the previous year.

Figure 1.15: Waterfall plot showing 2023 to 2024 changes in Ireland’s energy demand by sector


In terms of energy product breakout, just over half (54.3%) of energy demand in 2024 was sa tisfied
through end -user consumption of oil products, mainly petrol, diesel, and heating oils. Electricity
accounted for just under a quarter (22. 9%) of end-user energy demand in 202 4, followed by gas
demand (14.4%). For more detailed breakdowns on energy demand by sector , see section 5.2.

Figure 1.16: Breakout of 2024 energy demand across energy products . The term ‘NRW’ stands for
non-renewable wastes


As noted previously, Ireland’s energy demand in 2024 was 3.32 TWh higher than the previous year. At
the energy product level , there w as increase d end -user demand for gas, electricity , direct -use
renewable energy4, and oil . These increases were coupled with decrease demand for peat, non -
renewable waste, and coal. For more detailed breakdowns on energy demand by product , see section
5.1.

Figure 1.17: Waterfall plot showing 2023 to 2024 changes in Ireland’s energy demand by product.
The term ‘NRW’ stands for non -renewable wastes


Ireland’s end -user d emand for renewable e nergy is satisfied using both ‘standalone ’ renewable energy
product s, like biomass ( e.g. wooden logs and wood pellets ), and ‘blended ’ renewable energy product s.
An example of a blended energy product is ‘road diesel’ , which is the diesel fuel mix available for
purchase at garage forecourts across the country. Road diesel is a blend of 100% fossil -based
petrochemical diesel and some fraction of sustainable biofuel components, including fatty acid methyl
esters (FAME), hydro -treated vegetable oil (HVO), and co -processed HVO. The biofuel blending of these
sustainable compounds into road diesel helps reduces transport emissions by substituting -out s ome
fraction of the fossil -based die sel.




4 Direct -use renewable energy refers to renewable energy sub -products that are directly consumed by an end -user, such biomass
burned for heating in a home, or biodiesel used as fuel in a vehicle . It specifically excludes the renewable share of electricity
consumed by end users , as that is classified under renewable electricity supply rather than direct end-use of renewable energy.

Energy in Ireland

Similarly, Ireland’s road petrol also consists of a blend of fossil -based petrol and bioethanol , and our
‘grid gas’ has recently contain ed a blend of fossil gas and biomethan e, all helping reduce emissions
from those fuels. The renewable energy content of road diesel, road petrol, and grid gas in 2024 were
9.0%, 6.0%, and 0.1% respectively.

Figure 1.18: Renewable energy content of road diesel, road petrol, and grid gas in 2024


Biofuel blending into road diesel and road petrol has take n place for over a decade in Ireland, but
blending biomethane into grid gas is relatively new. Here, the REPowerEU -funded capital grants
scheme, launched by the Department of Agriculture, Food, and the Marine in September 2024, aims to
support the development of new biomethane plants and the conversion of biogas CHP plants for gas
upgrading and grid injection. The scheme is expected to enable 5 –7 plants, increasing biomethane
capacity by over 0.4 TWh annually in the coming year s. It is expected that t he biomethane produced
will be purchased primarily by suppliers to meet their obligations under the upcoming Renewable Heat
Obligation5.

The reduction in transport and heating emissions delivered by blending renewable energy into fossil
fuels is welcome . However, it is important to acknowledge that 91% of Ireland’s road diesel, 94% of its
road petrol, and 99.9% of its grid gas still c ame from fossil fuels in 2024 and acted as considerable
sources of Ireland’s emissions. For more details on blended road diesel and road petrol, see section s
1.4 and 5.2.2 . For more detail s on biomethane in grid gas, see section 4.4.1 .



5 www.gov.ie/en/department -of-climate -energy -and-the-environment/publications/renewable -heat -obligation/

Energy in Ireland

1.3 Supply and Demand of Electricity
Electricity accounted for just under a quarter ( 22.9%) of Ireland’s energy demand in 202 4, with just over
41% of that electricity coming from verified sustainable renewable sources. Renewable electricity is by
far the largest vector of renewable energy into Ireland’s homes, offices, and businesses.

1.3.1 Electricity Supply Trends
Ireland’s gross electricity supply in 202 4 was 36.3 TWh , up 4.2% on 2023 levels. Gross electricity supply
includes all electricity generation by utility -scale power plants (public thermal and CHP) as well as all
micro -to-small scale electricity generation, including rooftop solar PV. Gross supply includes the -net
import of electricity across international interconnectors but excludes the transmission and
distribution losses associated with moving electricity across the national grid, and the cycle -losses
incurred from energy storage, i.e. pumped or battery -storage.

In 202 4, 41.8% of Ireland’s gross electricity supply came from natural gas, followed by 3 2.1% from wind
generation, and 13.9 % from the net -import of electricity from Northern Ireland and Great Britain.
Combined these three sources accounted for 87. 9% of Ireland’s electricity supply.

In total, fossil fuel generation accounted for 4 5.3% of the electricity supply in 2024 – the lowest level on
record - with renewable generation accounting for 40. 0% of electricity supply. Other sources, consisting
of net -imports of electricity across international interconnectors and generation from non -renewable
wastes, accounted for the remaining 1 4.8%. On a percentage basis, Ireland’s electricity supply has
never had less fossil fuel generation or used more net -imported electricity.

Figure 1.19: Breakout of 2024 gross electricity supply by energy sub -products and type. The
‘other’ energy type includes non -fossil, non -renewable energy, like non -renewable wastes (NRW)
and net imports of electricity across international interconnectors


In absolute terms, Ireland’s gross electricity supply in 2024 was 1. 46 TWh higher than the previous year.
There w as a significant increase in net imports across internation al interconnectors (IC E lectr.) , and
smaller increases in PV, oil, biomass, renewable wastes, and biogas. These increases were coupled with
decrease s in generation from coal, wind, hydro, gas, peat , non -renewable wastes, and landfill (LF) gas.

Ireland’s renewable electricity supply in 2024 increased by 0.23 TWh or 1.6% in 2024. This net increase
in renewable electricity supply from increases in solar PV (+ 0.45 TWh) , biomass (+0.17 TWh), renewable
wastes, and biogas, coupled with decre ases from wind ( -0.21 TWh) , hydro ( -0.18 TWh), and landfill gas .


Energy in Ireland

Figure 1.20: Waterfall plot showing 2023 to 2024 changes in gross electricity supply by sub -
product. The term ‘NRW’ stands for non -renewable wastes


As noted above, a significant fraction of Ireland’s gross electricity supply in 2024 came from net -
imported electricity across interconnector s. Based on monthly data from January to September 2025 , it
is apparent that the fraction of electricity supply from net imports has further increas ed in 2025. The
best estimate from SEAI, based on an extrapolation of January -September data to the end of 2025 , is
that net -imported electricity may account for over 16% of Ireland’s utility scale electricity supply in
2025.

Figure 1.21: Monthly imports, exports, and net imports of electricity across international
interconnectors with Northern Ireland and Great Britain


The Greenlink interconnector, connecting Wexford with Wales, entered its ‘commercial operations’
phase at the beginning of 2025, with capacity available through the implicit S EM-GB ‘Intra Day 1
Auction’ from 29th January 2025. Since then, Ireland has three international interconnect ors to
Northern Ireland and Great Britain :

• East-West interconnector
• North -South inter connector6
• Greenlink interconnector



6 The North -South interconnector is the aggregate of four circuits that connect the two systems.

Energy in Ireland

These interconnectors allow for international trade and balancing of electricity flows to -and-from
Ireland and Northern Ireland (within the integrated Single Electricity Market, or I -SEM) and mainland
Great Britain (outside the I -SEM)7. In the period of January to September 2025, 45.9 % of Ireland’s net
imports of electricity flowed across the Greenlink interconnector, with the remaining 28.7% and 25.4%
of net imports flowing across the North -South and East -West interconnectors, respectively.

Figure 1.22: Monthly imports, exports, and net imports of electricity in 2025 across the North -
South, East -West, and Greenlink interconnectors


Trends in the relative contribution of net -imported electricity to wards Ireland’s electricity supply can be
more fully explored using utility -scale gross electricity supply data , which is available monthly. Utility -
scale gross supply covers electricity from large -scale grid -connected generators and accounts for
approximately 95% of all electricity supply , so it is an excellent proxy for total electricity supply.

This monthly data shows that Ireland has consistently made use of relatively high levels of net imports
since Q1-2023 . In 23 of the 24 calendar months from October 2023 to September 2025, Ireland has net
imported 10% or more of its gross electricity supply . In June 2025, net imports accounted for almost
one quarter (23.8%) of Ireland’s utility -scale gross electricity supply, and in May 202 5 net imports
accounted for more electricity supply than wind generation.



7 The Moyle interconnector connects the I -SEM in Northern Ireland with the mainland Great Britain market. As the endpoints of
this interconnector are not within the national boundary of the Republic of Ireland, electricity flows across this interconne ctor a re
not explicitly captured in Irish national statistics.

Figure 1.23: Monthly utility scale electricity supply in Ireland broken out by indigenous
generation and net imports across international interconnectors


In the above figure, where the data shows values greater than 100%, this indicates that Ireland was a

produced all its own electricity supply and more.

1.3.1.1 Wind Supply
Ireland’s wind generation comes from the thousands of individual wind turbines installed a cross the
country. Ireland’s installed wind capacity in 2024 was 4.94 GW, up 4.3% on the previous year. Ireland ’s
2025 CAP target for installed wind capacity is 6 GW, and its 2030 CAP targets for onshore and offshore
capacity are 9 GW and 5 GW, respectively. SEAI’s projections under the ‘with additional measures’
(WAM) scenario indicate a total installed capacity of 9.8 GW by the end of 2030. This WAM scenario is
the mo st optimistic reporting scenario modelled, achieving 68% RES -E by 20308.


Figure 1.24: Installed wind capacity with climate action plan (CAP) targets for 2025 and 2030, and
‘with additional measures’ (WAM) capacity projections





8 www.seai.ie/sites/default/files/publications/National -Energy -Projections -Report -2025.pdf

Energy in Ireland

Over the last 10 -years, Ireland has added wind capacity at an average rate of 0.2 7 GW per annum,
although this has dropped to a rate of 0.1 6 GW over the last 5 -years. To align to the pace of the WAM
scenario , the roll -out rate of onshore wind capacity needs to return to that previously achieved
between 2016 and 2019 . In addition, offshore wind projects delivering annual increases of between 0.8
GW and 1.4 GW in installed capacity need to be in place before the end of 2030. For more detail s on
wind capacity , see section 4.2.7 .

Figure 1.25: Year -to-year increases in installed wind capacity to 2024, and year -to-year increases
in WAM capacity projections of capacity from 2025 to 2030


Ireland generated 11. 65 TWh of renewable energy in 2024 from wind, down 1.8% on the previous
record of 11.87 TWh set in 202 3. Annual wind generation values over the last 5 years (2020 to 2024)
have been relatively consistent. For four of the last five years, Ireland’s wind generation has varied
between 11.40 TWh and 11.8 7 TWh , which is only a ±2% variation . In contrast, wind generation in the
preceding 5 years (201 6 to 2020) show ed an average annual increase of over 20 %. For more detail s on
wind generation , see section 4.2.

Figure 1.26: Annual wind generation and dispatch down values figures




Energy in Ireland

Multiple factors can impact the level of wind generation each year – the installed capacity, the average
quality of the wind, and the ‘dispatch down’ applied to wind generation due to constraint and
curtailment on the local and national grid. While a nationally representative wind quality figure is
difficult to determine, Eir Grid provides precise monthly constraint and curtailment statistics9 for wind
generation , where:

• Constraint is a reduction in allowed wind output due to local or network -specific grid limitations
(e.g. when part of the system cannot safely carry the wind power available, due to line, transformer,
or node limits)
• Curtailment is a reduction in allowed wind output due to system wide limitations ( e.g. when too
much non -synchronous generation would be online relative to demand or stability limits , i.e. the
safe system non-synchronous penetration level )

This data from Eir Grid shows an approximately 50:5 0 split across constraint and curtailment dispatch
down in the last 5 years, and a general ly increasing trend in dispatch down . The average annual level of
dispatch down for the last 5 years (2020 to 2024) was 9.2%, compared to 4.7% for the preceding 5 years
(2015 to 20 19). Future levels of d ispatch down c ould be reduced through upgrades and expansion s of
the electricity grid, increased flexibility in electricity demand -side management, and increased capacity
for energy storage and deployment.

1.3.1.2 Solar PV Supply
Ireland generated 1.09 TWh of renewable energy from solar PV in 2024 , up 69.1 % on the previous
record set in 2023. The rapid increase in sola r PV generation since 2022 has come from a step -change
increase in the connection of utility -scale ‘solar farms ’ to the national grid , coupled w ith near
exponential increases in embedded ‘rooftop’ solar PV on homes, businesses, and farms. In 202 4,
almost exactly two-thirds (66.8%) of Ireland’s solar PV generation came from utility -scale solar -farms.

Figure 1.27: Time -series of Ireland’s solar PV generation and a breakout of 2024 solar PV
generation


The strong seasonal variation of solar PV is clearly apparent in the monthly generation data from utility -
scale solar farms . The seasonal profile of solar PV is complementary to that of wind generation. S olar
PV peaks during the summer months, when wind is at its lowest, and wind peaks during the winter
months, when solar is at its lowest. A balanced blend of solar PV and wind capacity therefore acts to
smooths the seasonal -level variation associated with renewable electricity generation, reducing the
need for dispatchable fossil fuel generation.

Figure 1.28: Monthly solar PV generation from utility -scale grid -connected solar farms. The blue
line is a 12 -month moving point average (MPA) to illustrate the average trend

higher again than in 2024. Extrapolati ng January -September 2025 data to the end of 2025 , SEAI’s best
estimate is that utility -scale solar PV generation will be 1.1 TWh in 2025, up over 50% on 2024 levels.
Total solar PV generation in 2025 could then reach approximately 1.6 TWh.

1.3.2 Electricity Demand Trends
Ireland’s electricity demand in 2024 was 32.9 TWh , up 4.1% on the previous year. The three leading
demand sectors for electricity in 2024 were commercial services (41.2 %), residential (2 5.5%), and
industry ( 21.1 %). Only 1.3% of electricity demand in 2024 came from the transport sector – from the
national fleet of electric vehicles, the LUAS , and DART commuter services .

Figure 1.29: Breakout of 2024 electricity demand across economic sectors


In absolute terms, Ireland’s electricity demand in 2024 was 1.29 TWh higher than the previous year.
There were increases in electricity demand from all economic sectors, except agriculture and fisheries ,
which was down 0.00 1 TWh or 0.2% on the previous year. The largest sectoral increase in electricity
demand in 2024 came from the commercial services sector, which increased by 0.74 TWh or 5. 8% on
the previous year. The commercial services sector includes the ICT sub -sector , which includes data
centre demand .

Figure 1.30: Waterfall plot showing 2023 to 2024 changes in electricity demand by sector


Ireland’s electricity demand has grown every year for the last 10 years. A key driver of this increased
electricity demand has been Ireland’s data centres. Data published by the CSO10 shows that Ireland’s
data centre electricity demand in 2024 was 7.0 TWh , up 10.0 % on the previous year . In 202 4, data
centres accounted for just over one -fifth (2 1.2%) of all electricity demand in Ireland. For reference,
when it announced a new scheme for rating the sustainability of data centres in 2024, the European
Commission noted that data centres accounted for approximately 3% of the EU’s electricity demand.

Energy in Ireland

1.4 Transport Energy Demand
Transport has the highest energy demand of any sector of the Irish economy . In 202 4, the transport
sector accounted for 4 2.3% of Ireland’s energy demand, 37.7% of energy -related emissions, and 21 .7%
of Ireland’s total GHG emissions12.

Private car use accounted for 41.4 % of transport energy demand in 202 4. International aviation
accounted for a further 21.3% of transport energy demand , followed by heavy good vehicle (HGV) road
freight demand at 16.5%. In total, ‘road transport ’ accounted for just over three -quarters (75.7%) of
Ireland’s transport energy demand.

Figure 1.32: Breakout of 2024 transport demand by sub -sector13 and type


Energy demand from all three of the largest sub -sectors of transport – private car use, international
aviation, and HGV road freight – are increasing. Energy demand from private car use was 25.1 TWh in
2024, up by 3.2% on the previous year. Energy demand from the national fleet of HGVs was 10.0 TWh in
2024, up 7.9% on the previous year. Energy demand from both private car and HGVs was higher in
2024 than in 2019, the last calendar year before COVID impacts. This indicates that their energy
demand has now fully rebounded from COVID impacts.

Figure 1.33: Time -series of Ireland’s energy demand for private car use and road freight

Energy demand for aviation was 13.0 TWh in 2024, relatively unchanged compared to 13.3 TWh in

of jet kerosene for aviation in 2025 will be higher than in 2024. Based on an extrapolation of January -

then Ireland’s aviation e nergy demand in 202 5 will be higher than in 2019, indicat ing that aviation
energy demand has now fully rebounded from COVID impacts.

Figure 1.34: Monthly jet kerosene deliveries for aviation energy demand. The blue line is a 12 -

In terms of energy product breakout, over half ( 56.5 %) of transport energy demand in 20 24 was
satisfied using petrochemical diesel , i.e. the 100% fossil fuel component in blended road diesel . A
further 21.5 % of transport energy demand was satisfied using jet kerosene, which is currently 100%
fossil fuel based . Overall, 9 3.0% of Ireland’s transport energy demand in 2024 was satisfied using fossil
fuels in 202 4, with renewable energy and electricity only satisfying 6.3% and 0.7% of that demand ,
respectively .

Figure 1.35: Breakout of 2024 transport demand by energy sub -product and type


Renewable energy in transport is mainly delivered through the blending of fatty acid methyl esters
(FAME), hydro -treated vegetable oil (HVO), and co -processed HVO into petrochemical diesel, and
bioethanol in petrochemical gasoline. The resulting blended products of ‘road diesel’ and ‘road petrol’
are the fuels available for purchase at garage forecourts across the country .

Energy in Ireland

In 2024, 9.0% of road diesel’s energy content came from biofuels, up from 7.9% in 202 3. Based on
monthly survey returns from January to September 202 5, SEAI ’s best estimate is that 9. 5% of road
diesel energy content will come from biofuel in 202 5, setting a further record high.

After remaining relatively constant around 3.2% from 2016 to 2022, 6.0% of Ireland’s road petrol energy
content came from biofuels in 2024 , up from 4.2% in 2023. Based on monthly surveys returns from
January to September 202 5, SEAI’s best estimate is that 6.5% of road petrol energy content will come
from biofuel in 2025, setting a further record high.

Figure 1.36: Ireland’s annual biofuel energy blending rates into road diesel and road petrol. The
2025 value, highlighted in yellow text, is a best estimate from SEAI, based on monthly data from
January to September 2025


While biofuel blending can help reduce transport emissions, it is only a partial solution. Internationally,
there is limited availability of sustainable biofuel feedstock, and unchecked demand for biofuel
production crops can act to displace crops needed f or food production. Additionally, there are technical
limits on the blend -rate of certain biofuels in standard road vehicles. For example, bioethanol is limited
to 10% by volume in EN 228 gasoline, and conventional FAME -based biodiesel is limited to 7% by
volume in EN 590 diesel.

For more detail s on transport demand , see section s 5.2.2 and 6.3.




Energy in Ireland

1.5 Heat Demand
For the purposes of national energy statistics, and to align with European energy reporting, heat
demand is defined as total energy demand, less electricity demand, less transport demand. Heat
demand accounted for over 35.1 % of Ireland’s energy demand in 202 4.

1.5.1 Overall Heat Demand
Ireland’s heat demand in 2024 was 50.6 TWh, up 4.7% on the previous year. In 2024, the three
economic sectors with greatest heat demand were residential (44.1%), industry (36.5%), and
commercial services ( 7.3%). Combined, the residential and industry sectors account for 80.6% of
national heat demand.

Figure 1.37: Breakout of 2024 overall heat demand across economic sectors


In absolute terms, Ireland’s heat demand in 2024 was 2.26 TWh higher than the previous year. There
were increases in heat demand across all sectors of the economy, led by an increase of 1.27 TWh from
the residential sector.

Figure 1.38: Waterfall plot showing 2023 to 2024 changes in heat demand by sector


In terms of energy product breakout, 43.2% of heat demand in 2024 was satisfied through end -user
consumption of oil product s like kerosene and gasoil . Gas accounted for another 40.4% of heat
demand in 2024 . In 2024, only 8.5% of Ireland’s heat demand was satisfied using renewable energy.


Energy in Ireland

Figure 1.39: Breakout of 2024 overall heat demand across energy products


Ireland’s heat demand remains dominated by fossil fuels. In 202 4, 89.8 % of heat demand was satisfied
using fossil fuels. However, the proportion of fossil fuels satisfying heat demand is falling slowly - down
from 9 2.7% in 2014.

Figure 1.40: Time -series breakout of Ireland’s overall heat demand by energy type. The ‘other’
energy type includes non -fossil, non -renewable energy, like non -renewable wastes


Historically, most renewable energy satisfying Ireland’s heat demand ca me from biomass. In 20 14,
biomass accounted for almost three -quarters (74.3%) of the renewable energy sati sfying heat demand.
In 2024, biomass account ed for only 41.9% of renewable energy satisfying heat demand, with 28.0%
coming from the ambient heat captured by heat pumps , and 7.0% coming from biodiesel.

Energy in Ireland

1.5.2 Residential Heat Demand
Ireland’s residential heat demand in 2024 was 22.3 TWh, up 6.0% on the previous year. In terms of
energy product breakout, well over half ( 56.5%) of residential heat demand in 2024 was satisfied
through oil products, mainly kerosene . Gas accounted for almost exactly one -quarter (25.1%) of
residential heat demand in 2024, followed by peat (6.7%).

Figure 1.42: Breakout of 2024 residential heat demand across energy products, and a breakout
of residential electricity demand by consumption activity


Since heat demand is defined as total energy demand, less electricity demand, less transport demand ,
the 2024 residential heat demand value of 22.3 TWh does not include the 2024 residential electricity
demand value of 8.4 TWh. Based on SEAI's modelling of energy end use in households using the
dwelling energy assessment procedure (DEAP) and CSO weightings of housing architypes, SEAI
estimates that 21% of residential electricity demand was used for heating in 2024.

Renewable energy satisfied only 6.3% of residential heat demand in 2024. Ambient heat captured by
heat pumps accounted for almost two -thirds (64.7%) of the renewable residential heat demand in
2024, followed by 23.8% from biomass (e.g. wood logs, wood pellets, etc.), and 11.5% from solar
thermal heating. At the end of 2024, t here were approximately 153,000 homes with heat pumps , up
from 119,000 at the end of 202 414.

As noted previously, Ireland’s residential heat demand in 2024 was up 6.0% on the previous year ,
having previously decreased every year for the last 3 years. Residential heat demand is driven by many
different interconnected factors:
1. The a ffordability of energy prices , particularly oil and gas
2. Weather effects , particular the length and average temperature of the winter season
3. Societal and b ehavioural shifts , such as the return -to-office vs. work -from -home balance
4. The impact of new builds and energy upgrades on the heating efficiency of the housing stock
5. Demographic shifts in occupation of housing stock





14 Figures extracted from SEAI’s BER database.

Energy in Ireland

Figure 1.43: Time -series of Ireland’s residential heat demand


Weather effects play a particularly strong role in driving year -to-year changes in residential heat
demand , because they influence how often and how long home -heating systems are run . A simple
high -level metric for quantifying how often and how intensely homes around Ireland need to be heated
during the year is the sum of heating degree days ( HDDs ) below 15.5 °C in that year. Met Éireann
publishes monthly HDD data from multiple weather stations around Ireland.

There is a strong correlation between year -to-year changes in residential heat demand and year -to-
year changes in the number of HDDs . For example, the percentage changes in the number of HDDs for
2023 and 2024 were -6.1% and +5.7%, respectively, while the corresponding changes in residential heat
demand were -5.6% and +6.0%, respectively . In general , the number of HDDs in a year is a strong
predictor for residential heat demand in that year.

Figure 1.44: Comparison of year -to-year changes in residential heat demand and year -to-year
changes in the number of heating degree days (HDDs)




Energy in Ireland

However, t he simple correlation between the number of HDDs and residential heat demand is not
perfect , especially where other factors strong ly impact on heat demand . For example, 2020 was only
marginally colder having only 0.4% more HDDs compared to 2019, residential heat demand in 2020
increased by 9.7% . This was likely due to the strong impact of work -from -home and school -from -home
mandates with the onset of COVID. Conversely, residential energy demand decreased by 14.6% in 202 2,
despite the number of HDDs decreasing by only 6.8% . This was likely due to the impact of increased
energy prices in 2022 , when the cost of kerosene and gas approximately doubled.

Based on the 2025 HDD data available from Met Éireann , and the January to September 2025 monthly
data on residential gas demand , and monthly data on kerosene deliveries15 available to SEAI , it seems
likely that residential heat demand in 2025 will be lower than in 202 4. However, given the uncertainty
of the heating season in Q4 -2025 , it is not currently possible to confidently generate a best estimate
value.

For more detail s on heat demand , see section 6.2.






15 Approximately 95% of kerosene deliveries go to the residential market

Energy in Ireland

1.6 Ireland’s EU Energy Targets
Ireland has 2030 EU energy targets on both the share of renewable energy in its energy portfolio, and
the total energy demand of that portfolio. These dual targets act together to improve the quality of
energy in Ireland, by increasing the share of renewable energy being used, and to reduce the quantity
of energy needed in Ireland, by increasing efficient use of energy through better technology and
behaviours.

1.6.1 Renewable Energy Directive Targets
Ireland’s renewable energy share, and that of all EU member states, is calculated and monitored by the
European Commission under different iterations of the Renewable Energy Directive (RED). The
renewable energy share of EU member states is monitored throu gh four key metrics:

• RES-Overall Overall renewable energy share.
• RES-E Renewable energy share in electricity.
• RES-T Renewable energy share in transport.
• RES-H Renewable energy share in heating (and cooling).
The methodology for calculating the four RES results and setting their targets, which can include
applying multipliers, limits, normalisations, and sustainability -checks on specific energy sub -products, is
specified through the relevant iteration of the Di rective for a given year. Ireland’s RES -overall targets
out to 2030 are specified in the National Energy Climate Plan (NECP), prepared under the EU Regulation
on the Governance of the Energy Union, RED II and RED III. In addition to a 2020 ‘baseline’ RES -Overall
target of 16% in all years to 2030, Ireland has a series of reference points for specific milestone years:

• 2022 19.3% (RED -II).
• 2025 27.6% (RED -III).
• 2027 33.6% (RED -III).
• 2030 43.0% (RED -III).
In 202 4, Ireland’s RES -Overall result was 16.1 %, up from 1 5.2% in 202 3. Ireland’s RES -overall result for
2024 was the highest on record , without the use of statistical transfers , and just over our 2020
‘baseline’ target of 16% . However, Ireland’s RES -overall result for 2024 is significantly below the
indicative trajectory set by the milestone years reference points set out in the NECP.

Figure 1.45: Ireland’s overall renewable energy share (RES -Overall) results and targets




Energy in Ireland

Although Ireland has no binding national target for RES -E for 2030, high level s of renewable energy
share in electricity (RES -E) play a key part in Ireland’s strategy to achieve its RES-overall target for 2030.
Ireland’s Climate Action Plan 202 5 (CAP 202 5) maintains a long -standing target to increase the RES-E to
80% by 2030. In 202 4, Ireland’s RES -E result was 41.3 %, up from 40.4 % in 202 3. Ireland’s RES -E result in
2024 was the highest on record but needs to almost double by 2030 to reach the 80% target .

Over the last 5 years (2020 to 2024) , Ireland’s RES-E result s have been relatively consistent , varying by
just ±1.8 percentage points around a central value of 39.5%. In contrast, over the preceding 5 years
(2016 to 2020) , Ireland’s RES -E result increased by 12.0 percentage points . The apparent plateauing of
the RES -E results over the last 5 -years is due to several factors:

1. A plateauing of wind generation , which is the largest contributor to renewable electricity , over the
last 5 years .
2. A methodology -change in the RES -E calculation applied in 2021 under RED -II, introduc ing formal
sustainability checks on specific renewable energy products, i.e. biomass and biogas .
3. Demand for e lectricity increasing faster than the roll-out rate of renewable generation capacity, i.e.
increases in the denominator of the RES -E calcula tion (total electricity supply) offsetting increas es in
the numerator of the RES -E calculation (renewable electricity supply).

Figure 1.46: Ireland’s renewable energy share of Electricity (RES -E) results and target


For more details on Ireland’s RES results and targets , including RES -Transport and RES -Heating , see
section 8.2.

1.6.2 Energy Efficiency Directive Targets
Under Article 4 of the Energy Efficiency Directive (EED), the EU has set itself a binding target of reducing
total final energy consumption by 763 Mtoe by 2030. In February 2024, the Irish government approved
Ireland’s Article 4 target under the EED and su bmitted this as part of Ireland’s NECP . Under its
approved Article 4 EED target, Ireland is required to make sincere cooperative efforts to limit its total
final energy consumption16 (TFEC -EED) to 10.451 Mtoe by 2030.

Ireland’s TFEC -EED result in 2024 was 12.256 Mtoe , up 2.2% on the previous year. To a chiev e the 2030
TFEC -EED target , Ireland will need to reduce its energy demand by 14.7% in the next 6 years, which is
equivalent to a 2.6% compounding annual reduction for the next 6 years.


16 The definition of the total final energy consumption used for Article 4 of the EED excludes the use of ambient heat captured by
heat -pumps and so differs slightly from the total final energy consumption cited in other statistics.

Energy in Ireland

The 2030 TFEC -EED target require s Ireland to reduc e its energy demand to its lowest level in 25 years ,
below that observed in 2020 during the height of COVID impacts on the economy. Ireland’s progress
against its EED target is subject to monitoring by the European Commission through Ireland’s NECP.

Figure 1.47: Ireland’s total final energy consumption under the EU’s energy efficiency directive
(EED) and Ireland’s 2030 Article 4 target


In addition to the Article 4 EED target , which seek s to reduce absolute energy demand, Article 8 of the
EED requires Member States to achieve ‘new energy savings ’ targets each year that are equivalent to :

• 2021 -2023: 0.8% of TFEC
• 2024 -2025: 1.3% of TFEC
• 2026 -2027: 1.5% of TFEC
• 2028 -2030: 1.9% of TFEC

The Article 8 EED target s are binding on Member States , and the measures and savings must achieve
certain criteri a to be deemed countable towards those targets :

• Enduring – The energy saving must last over time.
• Material - The measure employed reported must be the reason for the energy savings.
• Additional – The measure must be over -and-above minimum standards required by EU law.
• Verifiable – The measure and savings must be monitored and recorded.






Energy in Ireland

2 Overview of energy data
2.1 The National Energy Balance
SEAI’s Energy Statistics team, within the Research and Policy Insights Directorate, is responsible for
collecting, analysing and reporting Ireland’s national energy statistics. It fulfils many of Ireland’s
obligations for reporting energy statistics to both Eurostat (EU Commission) and the International
Energy Agency (IEA).

Regulation (EC) No 1099/2008 on energy statistics, referred to as the Energy Statistics Regulation or
ESR, is the key EU legislation that sets out the requirements for compiling and reporting annual and
monthly national energy statistics.

SEAI Energy Statistics produces the National Energy Balance, the definitive source of annual data on the
supply, transformation, and demand of energy in Ireland. The balance is based on the direct surveying
of hundreds of energy suppliers or operators , as well as public administrative data from the Central
Statistics Office (CSO), Environmental Protection Agency (EPA), Department of Climate, Energy and the
Environment (DCEE), Revenue Commissioners, and others.

The NEB fulfils three primary roles:
• Provides key data to assist in policy making, investment decisions, and research.
• Is a key input to the EPA’s Greenhouse Gas (GHG) Inventory, and so directly informs national
emissions under the national carbon budgets, sectoral emission ceilings, EU Effort Sharing
Regulation and Ireland’s reporting to the UNFCCC.
• Provides the basis of Ireland’s reporting under the Energy Statistics Regulation, including
measurement of Ireland progress towards several targets under the Energy Efficiency Directive and
Renewable Energy Directive.

The information in this report is largely based on Ireland ’s 202 4 National Energy Balance [1] and the
preceding annual balances back to 1990 . These balances quantify the flow and transformation of
energy sources from primary supply to final consumption, and profile that consumption across
different sectors of the economy.

A simplified schematic for the energy balance flow is illustrated in Figure 2.1. The National Energy
Balances are the definitive sources for constructing other normalised national energy indicators, such
as energy intensity, energy efficiency, and energy -related greenhouse gas emissions. A Sankey diagram
showing the flow of energy in Ireland 202 4 is provided in Figure 2.2 .


Energy in Ireland

Figure 2.1: Main energy flows in Ireland



Figure 2.2: Energy Flow in Ireland in 202 4 (TWh)


The energy balance is organise d into three main parts , which are discussed in the following
subsections :

Energy in Ireland

• Primary energy – section 3 provides data on the production ( i.e. extraction of energy from natural
sources), imports , export and stock changes of energy in Ireland.
• Energy transformation – section 4 provides data on the transformation of energy from one to
another (e.g. electrical power generation, oil refining)
• Final energy consumption – section 5 provides data on the type and quantities of energy consumed
in each sector of the economy.
SEAI constructs the expanded National Energy Balance for each fuel or energy product, such as
gasoline, lignite , biomass and electricity . There are currently 3 6 energy produc ts listed in the expanded
balance , although some are no longer used in Ireland . The number of fuels and energy products
included in the balance has increased over the years as Ireland’s energy mix has diversified. For
analysis and presentation purposes, energy products can be combined into seven aggregated products
or energy types:
• Coal
• Peat
• Oil
• Natural gas
• Renewables
• Non -renewable waste
• Electricity

A complete list of the energy produc ts / sub -types that appear in the expanded National Energy
Balance and this report is provided in Appendix 10.

In general, the data in this report is presented for aggregated energy type s; however, where
appropriate a more complete breakdown is provided for each sub -type . Short version s of the energy
balance in units of terawatt -hours (TWh) and kilotonnes of oil equivalent (ktoe ) are presented in
Appendix 10. A more detailed, expanded balance showing data on each sub-energy type is available on
the SEAI website at https://www.seai.ie/data -and-insights/seai -statistics/key -publications/national -
energy -balance/ .

The data in the national energy balances are informed by survey responses received from
approximately 300 organisations, including energy producers, import/export companies and energy
supply companies. Besides populating the energy balance, SEAI uses these surveys to fulfil Ireland’s
energy statistics reporting obligations to Eurostat (EU Energy Statistics Regulation ( EC 1099/2008 )) [2]
and the International Energy Agency (IEA) .

To ensure that the best possible information is always available to users, SEAI employs a policy of
continuous improvement regarding historic energy balances , updating these with data revisions and
new methodologies as they become available. An overview of the revisions and amendments to energy
statistics made during 202 5 is provided in Appendix 8.

2.2 Additional data
To help policymakers, analysts, researchers, and the public better understand Ireland’s energy data,
SEAI has expanded its use of data visualisations and interactive dashboards. SEAI ’s interactive graphics
and dashboards allow users to explore monthly updates on energy delivery; quarterly updates on
energy and fuel costs; and geographical analysis. Please visit SEAI’s website at https://www.seai.ie/data -
and-insights/seai -statistics/monthly -energy -data/electricity -monthly/ to access the data.

SEAI currently publishes the energy balance for each year from 1990 to 202 4. However, for the
purposes of lending clarity to the data of most interest, the charts in this report generally only show

Energy in Ireland

data from 200 4 onwards, while the tables show comparisons back to 201 4. A spreadsheet containing
complete timeseries from 1990 to 202 4 for almost all graphs and charts in this report will be made
available for download via the link in Appendix 10 shortly after launch.

Feedback and comments on this report are welcome. Contact details are available on the back cover of
this report.

2.3 Data on energy -related emissions
For Energy in Ireland 2024, SEAI moved its energy -related emission reporting from mega tonnes of
carbon dioxide (MtCO 2) to mega tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MtCO 2eq). The greenhouse gases
(GHGs) covered within these CO 2eq estimates include carbon dioxide (CO 2), methane (CH 4) and nitrous
oxide (N 2O), gases which are known to contribute to the warming of the earth’s atmosphere to varying
extents over a given timeframe . GHG quantities from fuel combustion have been calculated using
sector and fuel specific data from the EPA’s National Inventory Submissions [3]. To calculate a total
CO 2eq value, t he emission metric used to quantify th e differences in cumulative warming effects
attributed to each of these gases is the 100 -year global warming potential (GWP 100) and has been
adapted from the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report . [4]

This change in methodology better aligns SEAI ’s reporting of energy -related emissions to those in
national carbon budgets, and the EP A’s GHG National Inventory Submission s, compiled in accordance
with IPCC guidelines.


Energy in Ireland

3 Primary energy



3.1 Primary energy production
Primary energy production refers to the production of energy products from natural sources within
Ireland’s national boundaries . Examples of production include extraction of peat, crude oil or natural
gas from natural deposits ; recovery of waste for energy purposes ; electricity generation by wind , solar
or hydro ; thermal energy collect ion by solar thermal systems ; and production of biomass or biogas.

Figure 3.1 shows the latest breakdown of Ireland ’s annual primary energy production by energy type .
Figure 3.2 shows Ireland ’s annual primary energy production by energy type and the ratio of primary
energy production to supply.
Primary energy is the total amount of energy that is required to satisfy the down -stream final
energy use of end -users. It describes the initial energy sources that are transformed, transmitted,
and distributed, before that energy is made available to end -users for final consumption. Primary
energy therefore describes the energy inputs of processes like electricity generation and crude oil
refining, rather than the consumption of that electricity or refined oil products.

The primary energy supply depends on the final energy use, as well as the efficiencies of the
various transformation processes needed to ensure that end -users receive final energy in the form
that is needed. Just like final energy use, primary energy suppl y can be usefully analysed by fuel,
sector and mode.

3.3 Primary energy supply and requirement
SEAI uses the following standard definition of primary energy supply for each fuel and energy type in
the energy balance :

Primary energy supply = primary energy production + imports – exports – int. marine bunkers + stock change



Energy in Ireland

Primary energy production is discussed in section 3.1, while i mports and exports are discussed in
section 3.2. International mari ne bunkers refer to the fuel delivered at Ireland’s ports to vessels that are
engaged in international navigation; currently only fuel oil and gas oil are used for this purpose. Stock
change is the change between opening and closing stock levels for a fuel held in Ireland during the
year. A positive stock change indicates the net effect of the stock of a product being drawn down to
enter the primary energy supply. A negative value indicates that the stock of the product has increased
during the year. Stocks do not include reserves of fuels or products that are not yet extracted from
natural d eposi ts.

Another key measure of primary energy is primary energy requirement, defined as primary energy
supply less non -energy use of the fuel or energy type :

Primary energy requirement = primary energy supply – non-energy use

For most fuel types included in the energy balance, there are no significant non-energy uses , and their
primary energy supply and requirement are equal . In Ireland, t he exceptions to this are bitumen,
lubricating oil and white spirits , all oil products not used for energy purposes. Some natural gas was
used for non -energy purposes up until 2002. Renewables used for materials, such as wood for
construction or peat for gardening are not tracked in the energy balance as they are not used for
energy purposes .

Figure 3.4 displays the breakdown of energy types in Ireland’s primary energy requirement for 202 4.

Figure 3.4: Share of p rimary energy requirement by energy type


Figure 3.5 shows the trend in annual total primary energy requirement by energy type . Table 3.5
provides a detailed summary of the quantities and share of each energy type in Ireland’s total primary
energy requirement in comparison to previous years.


Energy in Ireland

Total primary energy requirement peaked in 2008 before the economic downturn and reached a low in
2014 before growing to a relatively stable level from 2016 to 2019 . A significant contraction to total
primary energy requirement occurred in 2020 due to the impacts of the COVID -19 pandemic; this
contraction was mostly confined to oil products, caused by a downturn in transport demand.

After growing in 2021 and 2022, total primary energy requirement decreased in 2023, due to declining
use of natural gas . In 2024, it grew again , to near its pre-pandemic level.

In absolute terms, Ireland’s 2024 total primary energy requirement (168.04 TWh) was below that from
20 years ago (17 6.20 TWh) and above that from 10 years ago (15 4.07 TWh) , with intervening periods of
growth and decline. Neverth eless , the mix of fuels and energy types in primary energy has evolved
significantly during this time. The broad trend has been the growth of renewables and natural gas
displacing oil, coal and peat. Despite the meaningful development of renewables, fossil fuels still
domin ate Ireland’s primary energy supply.

Figure 3.5: Primary energy requirement by energy type

3.4 Security of supply and import depen dency
Energy security, in its simplest terms, means having uninterrupted access to reliable, affordable
supplies of energy. Secure supplies of energy are essential for our economy and for maintaining safe
and comfortable living conditions.

Energy import s dependency is one of the simplest and most widely used indicators of a country’s
energy security, with indigenous energy sources generally considered to be more secure than imported
energy. While the overall energy import s dependency figure provides a useful context, a deeper
understanding of energy security requires more detailed information on individual energy sources. This
includes the countries from where each fuel is sourced, global market conditions, transportation a nd
other infrastruc ture requirements. It also requires analysis of the current trends in energy use, and of
the significant changes that will occur in energy use - both nationally and globally over the coming
years. Energy security is considered in more detail in a separate SEAI publication s [5], [6].

Energy import s dependency is defined as the ratio of net energy imports to gross available energy [7]:
Energy import dependency = Imports −Exports
Gross available energy

Or:
Energy import dependency = Imports −Exports
Primary energy requirement +Non energy use +Int.marine bunkers

Figure 3.6 illustrates the trend in import dependency since 1990, comparing it with that for the EU 27,
and shows the dramatic change in Ireland’s import dependency in 2016 resulting from the start of
natural gas production from the Corrib gas field. Indigenous production accounted for 32% of Ireland’s
energy requirements in 1990. From the mid -1990s, impor t dependency grew significantly due to the
increase in energy use, together with the decline in indigenous natural gas production at Kinsale since
1995, and decreasing peat production. Ireland’s overall import dependency reached 91% in 2006. In
2016, import dependency fell sharply following the opening of the Corrib gas field but subsequently
increased again, as annual production from the field decrease d. Import dependency increased slightly

Energy in Ireland

again from 78% in 2023 to 80% in 2024 due to increased primary energy requirement and decrease d in
primary energy production .

This overall trend reflects the fact that Ireland does not have significant indigenous fossil fuel resources
and has only in recent years begun to harness significant quantities of renewable s.

Figure 3.6: Import dependency of Ireland and the EU -27

Source: SEAI and Eurostat


Energy in Ireland

4 Energy t ransformation


4.1 Overview of transformation
As shown in Figure 4.1, the two most significant energy transformation processes in Ireland are thermal
electricity generation and oil refining. Oil refining has maintained a relatively stable long -term average
input of around 37 TWh per year, with a decline in 2024 due to scheduled maintenance. Meanwhile, the
transformation input of public thermal power plants has shown a long -term downward trend, as coal,
oil, and peat -fired power are gradually replaced by gas, non-combustible renewables, and electricity
imports.

Figure 4.1: Energy inputs to transformation processes



Energy transformation involves converting one fuel type or energy source into another, such as
transforming crude oil into petrol and diesel in an oil refinery, or converting coal and gas into
electricity in a thermal generation plant. Approximately half of all primary energy undergoes a
transformation , of one kind or another , before it reaches an end user for final consumption.

Primary energy supply includes all inputs and losses in energy transformation processes, while final
energy use only includes the outputs from those transformation processes. Transformation outputs are
less than the primary supply inputs due to the energy required to make the transformations, and losses
from those processes.

Energy in Ireland

4.2 Electricity generation and supply
Total energy inputs to electricity generation account for approximately one -third of Ireland ’s total primary
energy supply. The S ankey diagram in Figure 4.2 shows the flow of energy from the inputs to electricity
generation through to the final electricity used by the different sectors. As the diagram shows , a
significant portion of the energy used to generate electricity is lost before the electricity reach es the end
user, through a combination of transformation losses, own use of electricity by power plants, energy
storage losses , and transmission and distribution losses.

Figure 4.2: Flow of energy in electricity generation and consumption (TWh)17


4.2.1 Energy input to electricity generation
Figure 4.3 shows the trends in energy input to electricity generation broken out by energy type.

Note that non-combustible renewable sources accounted for a higher share of generated electricity
than of energy input to electricity generation . This is because the thermal generation of electricity from
natural gas and coal has significant energy losses (see Figure 4.2), while electricity generation from non -
combustible renewable sources (wind, hydro and solar) is considered to be 100% efficient as this is the
point at which the measurable energy enters the system.

years.

4.2.2 Gross electricity production from generation
Electricity output from generation is recorded as gross electricity production in the National Energy
Balance. As defined by the IEA and Eurostat, gross electricity production is the amount of electrical
energy measured at the output terminals of the main generator or inverter and includes energy which
is used by plant auxiliaries and transformer losses within the site or station [8].

Electricity output from grid -connected energy storage, i.e. pumped hydroelectric or batteries, is not
included in the data presented in this section. See section 4.2.5 for details on energy inputs and
outputs for grid -connected energy storage.

Figure 4.5 and Table 4.3 show the gross electricity production from each energy sub -type ; this includes
electricity from all electricity producers, from residential rooftop solar PV to utility scale thermal power
plants and wind farms.
